    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… I’m here because I can’t get out from under this feeling that everything that goes wrong is somehow my fault. Like at work, if something is delayed or someone’s unhappy, my brain immediately goes to, “You messed this up, you’re not good at your job, you’re a bad employee.” And then at home it’s the same thing — if there’s a problem with the house or something I can’t keep up with, I start thinking I’m a bad homeowner too, like I don’t deserve to have the things I’ve worked for.

It sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but it’s like this constant guilt, like I’m waiting for proof that I’m going to lose everything because I deserve to. I just feel… worthless, I guess. Like I’ve failed in all these roles I’m supposed to be handling.

Yeah… I think it did start after something specific, or at least it got going around then. It was after I bought my townhouse. That was about… two years and three months ago, roughly.

And I think that’s when the guilt and the “I’m failing” thoughts really started becoming more constant. It wasn’t like one single day where everything changed, but looking back, that’s the point where it began.

Yes… yeah, I do. It’s not only the townhouse. That’s part of it, definitely, because it gave my brain this very concrete thing to latch onto, like, “If you can’t keep up with this, you’re failing.” But the feeling is bigger than that.

Like at work, if something goes wrong — even something that probably has multiple reasons or other people involved — I immediately feel like it’s because I did something wrong. Like I’m the weak link, or I’m not competent enough, or everyone is going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then it kind of spreads into this whole judgment of myself, like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, I’m a bad employee, a bad person, someone who doesn’t deserve what I have.

So, yes, even aside from the townhouse, there’s this… constant guilt, I guess. Like everything that goes wrong gets traced back to me somehow. And I keep feeling like I deserve to lose the things I worked for because maybe I didn’t really earn them, or I’m not handling them well enough. It’s exhausting, honestly, because even when nothing huge is happening, I still feel like I’m waiting for proof that I’m failing.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I can point to another clear, separate two-week stretch before this where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely the same kind of depressed most days,” or where I clearly lost interest in everything.

I mean, I’ve had times before where I was stressed or unhappy, like anyone, and I’m sure there were rough patches where I didn’t feel great. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Sleep has been… really bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed and I’m tired, like I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there awake for hours. I end up listening to every little noise in the house — the walls settling, pipes, whatever small creaks happen — and it feels like I’m just stuck there waiting for sleep to happen.

And then even if I do finally fall asleep, I usually wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.

I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.

There are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.

I haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. But no, I’m not sitting there making plans to hurt myself. It’s more these passive thoughts when I’m worn down.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t get out from under this feeling that everything that goes wrong is somehow my fault. Like at work, if something is delayed or someone’s unhappy, my brain immediately goes to, “You messed this up, you’re not good at your job, you’re a bad employee.” And then at home it’s the same thing — if there’s a problem with the house or something I can’t keep up with, I start thinking I’m a bad homeowner too, like I don’t deserve to have the things I’ve worked for.\n\nIt sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but it’s like this constant guilt, like I’m waiting for proof that I’m going to lose everything because I deserve to. I just feel… worthless, I guess. Like I’ve failed in all these roles I’m supposed to be handling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually avoid social situations because you’re afraid people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I usually avoid things for that reason. I mean… I don’t know, I can be uncomfortable wondering how I’m coming across, but I haven’t really thought of it as, like, “I can’t go because they’ll reject me.” It’s more that I get in my head afterward sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start after something specific, or at least it got going around then. It was after I bought my townhouse. That was about… two years and three months ago, roughly.\n\nAt first I thought it would feel like this big adult milestone, like something I should be proud of, and I was, kind of. But pretty quickly the pressure of keeping up with it started building — the payments, the maintenance, making sure nothing went wrong, feeling like if anything slipped it meant I wasn’t handling my life right. And I think that’s when the guilt and the “I’m failing” thoughts really started becoming more constant. It wasn’t like one single day where everything changed, but looking back, that’s the point where it began.\n\nDoctor: Aside from worries about the townhouse and keeping up with it, do you still feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I do. It’s not only the townhouse. That’s part of it, definitely, because it gave my brain this very concrete thing to latch onto, like, “If you can’t keep up with this, you’re failing.” But the feeling is bigger than that.\n\nLike at work, if something goes wrong — even something that probably has multiple reasons or other people involved — I immediately feel like it’s because I did something wrong. Like I’m the weak link, or I’m not competent enough, or everyone is going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then it kind of spreads into this whole judgment of myself, like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, I’m a bad employee, a bad person, someone who doesn’t deserve what I have.\n\nSo, yes, even aside from the townhouse, there’s this… constant guilt, I guess. Like everything that goes wrong gets traced back to me somehow. And I keep feeling like I deserve to lose the things I worked for because maybe I didn’t really earn them, or I’m not handling them well enough. It’s exhausting, honestly, because even when nothing huge is happening, I still feel like I’m waiting for proof that I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think that’s fair to say. It’s been going on a long time now — about two years and one month, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but the depressed feeling, and also just not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been there pretty consistently for that whole stretch. I end up listening to every little noise in the house — the walls settling, pipes, whatever small creaks happen — and it feels like I’m just stuck there waiting for sleep to happen.\n\nAnd then even if I do finally fall asleep, I usually wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.\n\nThere are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.\n\nI haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. But no, I’m not sitting there making plans to hurt myself. It’s more these passive thoughts when I’m worn down.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 27 / business_operations_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “too much,” because a lot of the things I think about feel like they have some reason behind them, even if maybe I go over them more than I need to.

Like, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff or whether I handled something wrong, or just general life things, but I don’t know that it’s across “many different parts” in the way you’re asking. It’s not like I’m constantly worrying about every possible thing all day long for no reason. At least I don’t think so.

Sometimes I probably overthink, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as this uncontrollable worry about everything. I guess I’d say I have stress and I dwell on things, but I’m not sure it’s the same as what you mean by excessive worrying.

I mean… I don’t know, I can be uncomfortable wondering how I’m coming across, but I haven’t really thought of it as, like, “I can’t go because they’ll reject me.” It’s more that I get in my head afterward sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start after something specific, or at least it got going around then. Like I’m the weak link, or I’m not competent enough, or everyone is going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then it kind of spreads into this whole judgment of myself, like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, I’m a bad employee, a bad person, someone who doesn’t deserve what I have.\n\nSo, yes, even aside from the townhouse, there’s this… constant guilt, I guess. Like everything that goes wrong gets traced back to me somehow. It’s been going on a long time now — about two years and one month, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but the depressed feeling, and also just not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been there pretty consistently for that whole stretch. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it’s changed, but it’s kind of messy. It hasn’t been one simple thing like “I’m never hungry” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s more like during the day I just don’t really eat proper meals. I’ll have coffee and maybe toast, or something small because it’s easy, and then I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t actually had lunch or anything that counts as real food.

Sometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until my clothes feel looser, like my waistband or work pants fitting differently. And then I’m like, okay, I guess I haven’t been eating normally. It’s not intentional exactly — I’m not trying to lose weight — it’s just that making food or deciding what to eat feels like too much, so I end up living on whatever is easiest. Coffee, toast, random snacks, that kind of thing.

nights I lie awake for hours listening to the house settle, then wake up around three or four and can’t get back to sleep before work.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: At work I move so slowly some mornings that opening spreadsheets, typing responses, or walking to a meeting feels like I’m pushing through wet cement.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I came in because I can’t stop feeling like everything that goes wrong is my fault, like I’m a bad employee and a bad homeowner who deserves to lose what I worked for.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During the same months I was barely eating, sleeping badly, and feeling like a failure, I was also hearing the woman’s voice and believing people were watching for mistakes with the house and my job.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: Looking back, the low mood and loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started, with only brief periods where I could function a little better.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood was not as heavy and I was getting through work more normally, but I still heard the voice near the vents and stayed convinced the mortgage company and my coworkers were monitoring me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve missed social plans, let the townhouse get messier than I can stand, and made enough mistakes at work that my supervisor started checking in on my performance. I’m still working, but it takes much more effort to keep up with basic responsibilities.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two years and three months, starting after I bought the townhouse and the pressure of keeping it began to build.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: The voices and fixed fears about being watched have been present for about a year and eight months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 2 year and 1 month\n  manifestation: The depression and loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 27 / business_operations_specialist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.\n\nThere are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.\n\nI haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. But no, I’m not sitting there making plans to hurt myself. It’s more these passive thoughts when I’m worn down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “too much,” because a lot of the things I think about feel like they have some reason behind them, even if maybe I go over them more than I need to.\n\nLike, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff or whether I handled something wrong, or just general life things, but I don’t know that it’s across “many different parts” in the way you’re asking. It’s not like I’m constantly worrying about every possible thing all day long for no reason. At least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes I probably overthink, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as this uncontrollable worry about everything. I guess I’d say I have stress and I dwell on things, but I’m not sure it’s the same as what you mean by excessive worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but it’s kind of messy. It hasn’t been one simple thing like “I’m never hungry” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s more like during the day I just don’t really eat proper meals. I’ll have coffee and maybe toast, or something small because it’s easy, and then I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t actually had lunch or anything that counts as real food.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until my clothes feel looser, like my waistband or work pants fitting differently. And then I’m like, okay, I guess I haven’t been eating normally. It’s not intentional exactly — I’m not trying to lose weight — it’s just that making food or deciding what to eat feels like too much, so I end up living on whatever is easiest. Coffee, toast, random snacks, that kind of thing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not totally sure. I mean, I feel tired a lot, but I don’t know if I’d honestly say it’s nearly every day in the way you mean. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t get out from under this feeling that everything that goes wrong is somehow my fault. Like at work, if something is delayed or someone’s unhappy, my brain immediately goes to, “You messed this up, you’re not good at your job, you’re a bad employee.” And then at home it’s the same thing — if there’s a problem with the house or something I can’t keep up with, I start thinking I’m a bad homeowner too, like I don’t deserve to have the things I’ve worked for.\n\nIt sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but it’s like this constant guilt, like I’m waiting for proof that I’m going to lose everything because I deserve to. I just feel… worthless, I guess. Like I’ve failed in all these roles I’m supposed to be handling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually avoid social situations because you’re afraid people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I usually avoid things for that reason. I mean… I don’t know, I can be uncomfortable wondering how I’m coming across, but I haven’t really thought of it as, like, “I can’t go because they’ll reject me.” It’s more that I get in my head afterward sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start after something specific, or at least it got going around then. It was after I bought my townhouse. That was about… two years and three months ago, roughly.\n\nAt first I thought it would feel like this big adult milestone, like something I should be proud of, and I was, kind of. But pretty quickly the pressure of keeping up with it started building — the payments, the maintenance, making sure nothing went wrong, feeling like if anything slipped it meant I wasn’t handling my life right. And I think that’s when the guilt and the “I’m failing” thoughts really started becoming more constant. It wasn’t like one single day where everything changed, but looking back, that’s the point where it began.\n\nDoctor: Aside from worries about the townhouse and keeping up with it, do you still feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I do. It’s not only the townhouse. That’s part of it, definitely, because it gave my brain this very concrete thing to latch onto, like, “If you can’t keep up with this, you’re failing.” But the feeling is bigger than that.\n\nLike at work, if something goes wrong — even something that probably has multiple reasons or other people involved — I immediately feel like it’s because I did something wrong. Like I’m the weak link, or I’m not competent enough, or everyone is going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then it kind of spreads into this whole judgment of myself, like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, I’m a bad employee, a bad person, someone who doesn’t deserve what I have.\n\nSo, yes, even aside from the townhouse, there’s this… constant guilt, I guess. Like everything that goes wrong gets traced back to me somehow. And I keep feeling like I deserve to lose the things I worked for because maybe I didn’t really earn them, or I’m not handling them well enough. It’s exhausting, honestly, because even when nothing huge is happening, I still feel like I’m waiting for proof that I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think that’s fair to say. It’s been going on a long time now — about two years and one month, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but the depressed feeling, and also just not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been there pretty consistently for that whole stretch. Some days I can function and get through what I need to do, so I almost second-guess whether I should call it “depressed,” but when I look at the pattern, yeah, it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current two-year period, had you ever gone through another distinct stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days or lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I can point to another clear, separate two-week stretch before this where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely the same kind of depressed most days,” or where I clearly lost interest in everything.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times before where I was stressed or unhappy, like anyone, and I’m sure there were rough patches where I didn’t feel great. This current period is the one that feels obvious and persistent enough that I can recognize it.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed and I’m tired, like I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there awake for hours. I end up listening to every little noise in the house — the walls settling, pipes, whatever small creaks happen — and it feels like I’m just stuck there waiting for sleep to happen.\n\nAnd then even if I do finally fall asleep, I usually wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.\n\nThere are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.\n\nThere are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.\n\nI haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. But no, I’m not sitting there making plans to hurt myself. It’s more these passive thoughts when I’m worn down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “too much,” because a lot of the things I think about feel like they have some reason behind them, even if maybe I go over them more than I need to.\n\nLike, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff or whether I handled something wrong, or just general life things, but I don’t know that it’s across “many different parts” in the way you’re asking. It’s not like I’m constantly worrying about every possible thing all day long for no reason. At least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes I probably overthink, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as this uncontrollable worry about everything. I guess I’d say I have stress and I dwell on things, but I’m not sure it’s the same as what you mean by excessive worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but it’s kind of messy. It hasn’t been one simple thing like “I’m never hungry” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s more like during the day I just don’t really eat proper meals. I’ll have coffee and maybe toast, or something small because it’s easy, and then I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t actually had lunch or anything that counts as real food.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until my clothes feel looser, like my waistband or work pants fitting differently. And then I’m like, okay, I guess I haven’t been eating normally. Yeah… I think maybe people have noticed, especially at work. I don’t know if anyone has said it in exactly those words, like “you’re moving slowly,” but I can feel it, and I think it probably shows.

Some mornings I’ll get to my desk and even basic things feel weirdly heavy and slow. Like opening up spreadsheets, clicking through files, typing out responses to emails—things I normally do without thinking—feel like I’m pushing through wet cement. Even walking to a meeting can feel like it takes more effort than it should, like my body just isn’t keeping up at the usual pace.

I’ve had moments where I’m aware people are waiting for me to answer or finish something, and I’m not trying to be difficult or distracted, it’s just like there’s this delay between knowing what I need to do and actually doing it. So… yes, I think at least at work it’s noticeable sometimes, even if people haven’t directly called it out.

Yeah… it has. I’m still functioning, like I’m still going to work and I’m not completely unable to take care of things, but it takes so much more effort than it used to.

At work, I’ve started making mistakes that I normally wouldn’t make — little things in reports or missing details in emails or not following through as cleanly on something. And it’s happened enough that my supervisor has started checking in more, like asking how projects are going or wanting to review things more closely. They haven’t threatened anything, but I can tell it’s noticeable, and that makes me feel embarrassed because I’m usually pretty dependable.

At home it’s similar. I live in a townhouse, and I’ve let it get messier than I can stand. Not like one bad day of dishes, more like piles of laundry, clutter, things staying where I dropped them because I can’t make myself deal with it right away. And then it bothers me every time I look at it, but actually fixing it feels like this huge project.

I’ve also missed social plans, which isn’t like me. I’ll say yes to something and then when it comes time to go, I just don’t have it in me, or I feel like I can’t pull myself together enough to show up and act normal. So… yes, it’s definitely made basic responsibilities harder. I’m keeping things going, but it feels like I’m doing it with a lot less margin than before.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F41.1, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t get out from under this feeling that everything that goes wrong is somehow my fault. Like at work, if something is delayed or someone’s unhappy, my brain immediately goes to, “You messed this up, you’re not good at your job, you’re a bad employee.” And then at home it’s the same thing — if there’s a problem with the house or something I can’t keep up with, I start thinking I’m a bad homeowner too, like I don’t deserve to have the things I’ve worked for.\n\nIt sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but it’s like this constant guilt, like I’m waiting for proof that I’m going to lose everything because I deserve to. I just feel… worthless, I guess. Like I’ve failed in all these roles I’m supposed to be handling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually avoid social situations because you’re afraid people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I usually avoid things for that reason. I mean… I don’t know, I can be uncomfortable wondering how I’m coming across, but I haven’t really thought of it as, like, “I can’t go because they’ll reject me.” It’s more that I get in my head afterward sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start after something specific, or at least it got going around then. It was after I bought my townhouse. That was about… two years and three months ago, roughly.\n\nAt first I thought it would feel like this big adult milestone, like something I should be proud of, and I was, kind of. But pretty quickly the pressure of keeping up with it started building — the payments, the maintenance, making sure nothing went wrong, feeling like if anything slipped it meant I wasn’t handling my life right. And I think that’s when the guilt and the “I’m failing” thoughts really started becoming more constant. It wasn’t like one single day where everything changed, but looking back, that’s the point where it began.\n\nDoctor: Aside from worries about the townhouse and keeping up with it, do you still feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I do. It’s not only the townhouse. That’s part of it, definitely, because it gave my brain this very concrete thing to latch onto, like, “If you can’t keep up with this, you’re failing.” But the feeling is bigger than that.\n\nLike at work, if something goes wrong — even something that probably has multiple reasons or other people involved — I immediately feel like it’s because I did something wrong. Like I’m the weak link, or I’m not competent enough, or everyone is going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then it kind of spreads into this whole judgment of myself, like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, I’m a bad employee, a bad person, someone who doesn’t deserve what I have.\n\nSo, yes, even aside from the townhouse, there’s this… constant guilt, I guess. Like everything that goes wrong gets traced back to me somehow. And I keep feeling like I deserve to lose the things I worked for because maybe I didn’t really earn them, or I’m not handling them well enough. It’s exhausting, honestly, because even when nothing huge is happening, I still feel like I’m waiting for proof that I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think that’s fair to say. It’s been going on a long time now — about two years and one month, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but the depressed feeling, and also just not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been there pretty consistently for that whole stretch. Some days I can function and get through what I need to do, so I almost second-guess whether I should call it “depressed,” but when I look at the pattern, yeah, it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current two-year period, had you ever gone through another distinct stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days or lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I can point to another clear, separate two-week stretch before this where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely the same kind of depressed most days,” or where I clearly lost interest in everything.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times before where I was stressed or unhappy, like anyone, and I’m sure there were rough patches where I didn’t feel great. But if you mean a distinct period that really stands out as depression for at least two weeks, before this current long stretch… I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind in a clear way. This current period is the one that feels obvious and persistent enough that I can recognize it.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed and I’m tired, like I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there awake for hours. I end up listening to every little noise in the house — the walls settling, pipes, whatever small creaks happen — and it feels like I’m just stuck there waiting for sleep to happen.\n\nAnd then even if I do finally fall asleep, I usually wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.\n\nThere are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.\n\nI haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. But no, I’m not sitting there making plans to hurt myself. It’s more these passive thoughts when I’m worn down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “too much,” because a lot of the things I think about feel like they have some reason behind them, even if maybe I go over them more than I need to.\n\nLike, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff or whether I handled something wrong, or just general life things, but I don’t know that it’s across “many different parts” in the way you’re asking. It’s not like I’m constantly worrying about every possible thing all day long for no reason. At least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes I probably overthink, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as this uncontrollable worry about everything. I guess I’d say I have stress and I dwell on things, but I’m not sure it’s the same as what you mean by excessive worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but it’s kind of messy. It hasn’t been one simple thing like “I’m never hungry” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s more like during the day I just don’t really eat proper meals. I’ll have coffee and maybe toast, or something small because it’s easy, and then I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t actually had lunch or anything that counts as real food.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until my clothes feel looser, like my waistband or work pants fitting differently. And then I’m like, okay, I guess I haven’t been eating normally. It’s not intentional exactly — I’m not trying to lose weight — it’s just that making food or deciding what to eat feels like too much, so I end up living on whatever is easiest. Coffee, toast, random snacks, that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I feel tired a lot, but I don’t know if I’d honestly say it’s nearly every day in the way you mean. Some days I can get through work and do what I need to do, even if I’m kind of dragging, and other days I notice it more, like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out whether it’s “low energy” or just being worn down, if that makes sense. I don’t wake up thinking, “I have no energy today,” every single day, but I do have stretches where I feel like I’m moving slower than normal or like normal tasks feel heavier. But I haven’t really been tracking it closely enough to say yes, definitely nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe people have noticed, especially at work. I don’t know if anyone has said it in exactly those words, like “you’re moving slowly,” but I can feel it, and I think it probably shows.\n\nSome mornings I’ll get to my desk and even basic things feel weirdly heavy and slow. Like opening up spreadsheets, clicking through files, typing out responses to emails—things I normally do without thinking—feel like I’m pushing through wet cement. Even walking to a meeting can feel like it takes more effort than it should, like my body just isn’t keeping up at the usual pace.\n\nI’ve had moments where I’m aware people are waiting for me to answer or finish something, and I’m not trying to be difficult or distracted, it’s just like there’s this delay between knowing what I need to do and actually doing it. So… yes, I think at least at work it’s noticeable sometimes, even if people haven’t directly called it out.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it harder for you to function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’m still functioning, like I’m still going to work and I’m not completely unable to take care of things, but it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nAt work, I’ve started making mistakes that I normally wouldn’t make — little things in reports or missing details in emails or not following through as cleanly on something. And it’s happened enough that my supervisor has started checking in more, like asking how projects are going or wanting to review things more closely. They haven’t threatened anything, but I can tell it’s noticeable, and that makes me feel embarrassed because I’m usually pretty dependable.\n\nAt home it’s similar. I live in a townhouse, and I’ve let it get messier than I can stand. Not like one bad day of dishes, more like piles of laundry, clutter, things staying where I dropped them because I can’t make myself deal with it right away. And then it bothers me every time I look at it, but actually fixing it feels like this huge project.\n\nI’ve also missed social plans, which isn’t like me. I’ll say yes to something and then when it comes time to go, I just don’t have it in me, or I feel like I can’t pull myself together enough to show up and act normal. So… yes, it’s definitely made basic responsibilities harder. I’m keeping things going, but it feels like I’m doing it with a lot less margin than before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I feel tired a lot, but I don’t know if I’d honestly say it’s nearly every day in the way you mean. I end up listening to every little noise in the house — the walls settling, pipes, whatever small creaks happen — and it feels like I’m just stuck there waiting for sleep to happen.\n\nAnd then even if I do finally fall asleep, I usually wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.\n\nI haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. Some days I can function and get through what I need to do, so I almost second-guess whether I should call it “depressed,” but when I look at the pattern, yeah, it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current two-year period, had you ever gone through another distinct stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days or lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I can point to another clear, separate two-week stretch before this where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely the same kind of depressed most days,” or where I clearly lost interest in everything.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times before where I was stressed or unhappy, like anyone, and I’m sure there were rough patches where I didn’t feel great. But if you mean a distinct period that really stands out as depression for at least two weeks, before this current long stretch… I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind in a clear way. This current period is the one that feels obvious and persistent enough that I can recognize it.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed and I’m tired, like I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there awake for hours. I end up listening to every little noise in the house — the walls settling, pipes, whatever small creaks happen — and it feels like I’m just stuck there waiting for sleep to happen.\n\nAnd then even if I do finally fall asleep, I usually wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.\n\nThere are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.\n\nI haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. But no, I’m not sitting there making plans to hurt myself. It’s more these passive thoughts when I’m worn down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “too much,” because a lot of the things I think about feel like they have some reason behind them, even if maybe I go over them more than I need to.\n\nLike, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff or whether I handled something wrong, or just general life things, but I don’t know that it’s across “many different parts” in the way you’re asking. It’s not like I’m constantly worrying about every possible thing all day long for no reason. At least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes I probably overthink, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as this uncontrollable worry about everything. I guess I’d say I have stress and I dwell on things, but I’m not sure it’s the same as what you mean by excessive worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but it’s kind of messy. It hasn’t been one simple thing like “I’m never hungry” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s more like during the day I just don’t really eat proper meals. I’ll have coffee and maybe toast, or something small because it’s easy, and then I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t actually had lunch or anything that counts as real food.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until my clothes feel looser, like my waistband or work pants fitting differently. And then I’m like, okay, I guess I haven’t been eating normally. It’s not intentional exactly — I’m not trying to lose weight — it’s just that making food or deciding what to eat feels like too much, so I end up living on whatever is easiest. Coffee, toast, random snacks, that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I feel tired a lot, but I don’t know if I’d honestly say it’s nearly every day in the way you mean. Some days I can get through work and do what I need to do, even if I’m kind of dragging, and other days I notice it more, like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out whether it’s “low energy” or just being worn down, if that makes sense. I don’t wake up thinking, “I have no energy today,” every single day, but I do have stretches where I feel like I’m moving slower than normal or like normal tasks feel heavier. But I haven’t really been tracking it closely enough to say yes, definitely nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe people have noticed, especially at work. I don’t know if anyone has said it in exactly those words, like “you’re moving slowly,” but I can feel it, and I think it probably shows.\n\nSome mornings I’ll get to my desk and even basic things feel weirdly heavy and slow. Like opening up spreadsheets, clicking through files, typing out responses to emails—things I normally do without thinking—feel like I’m pushing through wet cement. Even walking to a meeting can feel like it takes more effort than it should, like my body just isn’t keeping up at the usual pace.\n\nI’ve had moments where I’m aware people are waiting for me to answer or finish something, and I’m not trying to be difficult or distracted, it’s just like there’s this delay between knowing what I need to do and actually doing it. So… yes, I think at least at work it’s noticeable sometimes, even if people haven’t directly called it out.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it harder for you to function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’m still functioning, like I’m still going to work and I’m not completely unable to take care of things, but it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nAt work, I’ve started making mistakes that I normally wouldn’t make — little things in reports or missing details in emails or not following through as cleanly on something. And it’s happened enough that my supervisor has started checking in more, like asking how projects are going or wanting to review things more closely. They haven’t threatened anything, but I can tell it’s noticeable, and that makes me feel embarrassed because I’m usually pretty dependable.\n\nAt home it’s similar. I live in a townhouse, and I’ve let it get messier than I can stand. Not like one bad day of dishes, more like piles of laundry, clutter, things staying where I dropped them because I can’t make myself deal with it right away. And then it bothers me every time I look at it, but actually fixing it feels like this huge project.\n\nI’ve also missed social plans, which isn’t like me. I’ll say yes to something and then when it comes time to go, I just don’t have it in me, or I feel like I can’t pull myself together enough to show up and act normal. So… yes, it’s definitely made basic responsibilities harder. I’m keeping things going, but it feels like I’m doing it with a lot less margin than before.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when these symptoms were present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. I mean, if you’re asking whether there was at least a two-week stretch where it was there almost every day, then yes. It’s been much longer than that, honestly.\n\nThe depressed feeling and the not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been going on for about two years and maybe a month now. It hasn’t been identical every single day, like some days are a little more manageable than others, but it’s not like it came and went in just short bursts. There have absolutely been two-week periods where it was basically there every day, and honestly it feels more like it’s just been this long, ongoing thing that I’ve been trying to live around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… they are. I wish I could say I caught up and got it back to normal, but it’s still kind of ongoing.

It’s not like the place is unsafe or anything, but it’s messier than I’m comfortable with. There’s laundry that sits there longer than it should, clutter on counters, things I mean to put away and then they just stay where I dropped them. And every time I walk past it, I feel bad about it, but then actually doing it feels like another whole task I have to gear myself up for.

I’m still working, and I’m still handling the basics, but it takes a lot more effort than it used to. It’s the same kind of thing as at work, where I’m keeping up but barely — I’ve made enough mistakes that my supervisor has been checking in more, and at home it feels like I’m always behind. I’ve missed social plans too, because by the time I’m supposed to go, I feel like I can’t pull myself together and also deal with everything else.

So, yes… the townhouse stuff is still happening. It’s one of the things that makes me feel like I’m not really managing the way I used to.

Like at work, if something is delayed or someone’s unhappy, my brain immediately goes to, “You messed this up, you’re not good at your job, you’re a bad employee.” And then at home it’s the same thing — if there’s a problem with the house or something I can’t keep up with, I start thinking I’m a bad homeowner too, like I don’t deserve to have the things I’ve worked for.\n\nIt sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but it’s like this constant guilt, like I’m waiting for proof that I’m going to lose everything because I deserve to. It wasn’t like one single day where everything changed, but looking back, that’s the point where it began.\n\nDoctor: Aside from worries about the townhouse and keeping up with it, do you still feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I do. It’s not only the townhouse. That’s part of it, definitely, because it gave my brain this very concrete thing to latch onto, like, “If you can’t keep up with this, you’re failing.” But the feeling is bigger than that.\n\nLike at work, if something goes wrong — even something that probably has multiple reasons or other people involved — I immediately feel like it’s because I did something wrong. Like I’m the weak link, or I’m not competent enough, or everyone is going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then it kind of spreads into this whole judgment of myself, like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, I’m a bad employee, a bad person, someone who doesn’t deserve what I have.\n\nSo, yes, even aside from the townhouse, there’s this… constant guilt, I guess. Like everything that goes wrong gets traced back to me somehow. And I keep feeling like I deserve to lose the things I worked for because maybe I didn’t really earn them, or I’m not handling them well enough. It’s exhausting, honestly, because even when nothing huge is happening, I still feel like I’m waiting for proof that I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think that’s fair to say. It’s been going on a long time now — about two years and one month, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but the depressed feeling, and also just not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been there pretty consistently for that whole stretch. Some days I can function and get through what I need to do, so I almost second-guess whether I should call it “depressed,” but when I look at the pattern, yeah, it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current two-year period, had you ever gone through another distinct stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days or lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I can point to another clear, separate two-week stretch before this where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely the same kind of depressed most days,” or where I clearly lost interest in everything.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times before where I was stressed or unhappy, like anyone, and I’m sure there were rough patches where I didn’t feel great. But if you mean a distinct period that really stands out as depression for at least two weeks, before this current long stretch… I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind in a clear way. This current period is the one that feels obvious and persistent enough that I can recognize it.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed and I’m tired, like I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there awake for hours. I end up listening to every little noise in the house — the walls settling, pipes, whatever small creaks happen — and it feels like I’m just stuck there waiting for sleep to happen.\n\nAnd then even if I do finally fall asleep, I usually wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.\n\nThere are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.\n\nI haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. But no, I’m not sitting there making plans to hurt myself. It’s more these passive thoughts when I’m worn down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “too much,” because a lot of the things I think about feel like they have some reason behind them, even if maybe I go over them more than I need to.\n\nLike, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff or whether I handled something wrong, or just general life things, but I don’t know that it’s across “many different parts” in the way you’re asking. It’s not like I’m constantly worrying about every possible thing all day long for no reason. At least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes I probably overthink, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as this uncontrollable worry about everything. I guess I’d say I have stress and I dwell on things, but I’m not sure it’s the same as what you mean by excessive worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but it’s kind of messy. It hasn’t been one simple thing like “I’m never hungry” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s more like during the day I just don’t really eat proper meals. I’ll have coffee and maybe toast, or something small because it’s easy, and then I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t actually had lunch or anything that counts as real food.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until my clothes feel looser, like my waistband or work pants fitting differently. And then I’m like, okay, I guess I haven’t been eating normally. It’s not intentional exactly — I’m not trying to lose weight — it’s just that making food or deciding what to eat feels like too much, so I end up living on whatever is easiest. Coffee, toast, random snacks, that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I feel tired a lot, but I don’t know if I’d honestly say it’s nearly every day in the way you mean. Some days I can get through work and do what I need to do, even if I’m kind of dragging, and other days I notice it more, like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out whether it’s “low energy” or just being worn down, if that makes sense. I don’t wake up thinking, “I have no energy today,” every single day, but I do have stretches where I feel like I’m moving slower than normal or like normal tasks feel heavier. But I haven’t really been tracking it closely enough to say yes, definitely nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe people have noticed, especially at work. I don’t know if anyone has said it in exactly those words, like “you’re moving slowly,” but I can feel it, and I think it probably shows.\n\nSome mornings I’ll get to my desk and even basic things feel weirdly heavy and slow. Like opening up spreadsheets, clicking through files, typing out responses to emails—things I normally do without thinking—feel like I’m pushing through wet cement. Even walking to a meeting can feel like it takes more effort than it should, like my body just isn’t keeping up at the usual pace.\n\nI’ve had moments where I’m aware people are waiting for me to answer or finish something, and I’m not trying to be difficult or distracted, it’s just like there’s this delay between knowing what I need to do and actually doing it. So… yes, I think at least at work it’s noticeable sometimes, even if people haven’t directly called it out.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it harder for you to function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’m still functioning, like I’m still going to work and I’m not completely unable to take care of things, but it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nAt work, I’ve started making mistakes that I normally wouldn’t make — little things in reports or missing details in emails or not following through as cleanly on something. And it’s happened enough that my supervisor has started checking in more, like asking how projects are going or wanting to review things more closely. They haven’t threatened anything, but I can tell it’s noticeable, and that makes me feel embarrassed because I’m usually pretty dependable.\n\nAt home it’s similar. I live in a townhouse, and I’ve let it get messier than I can stand. Not like one bad day of dishes, more like piles of laundry, clutter, things staying where I dropped them because I can’t make myself deal with it right away. And then it bothers me every time I look at it, but actually fixing it feels like this huge project.\n\nI’ve also missed social plans, which isn’t like me. I’ll say yes to something and then when it comes time to go, I just don’t have it in me, or I feel like I can’t pull myself together enough to show up and act normal. So… yes, it’s definitely made basic responsibilities harder. I’m keeping things going, but it feels like I’m doing it with a lot less margin than before.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when these symptoms were present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. I mean, if you’re asking whether there was at least a two-week stretch where it was there almost every day, then yes. It’s been much longer than that, honestly.\n\nThe depressed feeling and the not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been going on for about two years and maybe a month now. It hasn’t been identical every single day, like some days are a little more manageable than others, but it’s not like it came and went in just short bursts. There have absolutely been two-week periods where it was basically there every day, and honestly it feels more like it’s just been this long, ongoing thing that I’ve been trying to live around.\n\nDoctor: Are the problems with the townhouse still ongoing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they are. I wish I could say I caught up and got it back to normal, but it’s still kind of ongoing.\n\nIt’s not like the place is unsafe or anything, but it’s messier than I’m comfortable with. There’s laundry that sits there longer than it should, clutter on counters, things I mean to put away and then they just stay where I dropped them. And every time I walk past it, I feel bad about it, but then actually doing it feels like another whole task I have to gear myself up for.\n\nI’m still working, and I’m still handling the basics, but it takes a lot more effort than it used to. It’s the same kind of thing as at work, where I’m keeping up but barely — I’ve made enough mistakes that my supervisor has been checking in more, and at home it feels like I’m always behind. I’ve missed social plans too, because by the time I’m supposed to go, I feel like I can’t pull myself together and also deal with everything else.\n\nSo, yes… the townhouse stuff is still happening. It’s one of the things that makes me feel like I’m not really managing the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t get out from under this feeling that everything that goes wrong is somehow my fault. Like at work, if something is delayed or someone’s unhappy, my brain immediately goes to, “You messed this up, you’re not good at your job, you’re a bad employee.” And then at home it’s the same thing — if there’s a problem with the house or something I can’t keep up with, I start thinking I’m a bad homeowner too, like I don’t deserve to have the things I’ve worked for.\n\nIt sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but it’s like this constant guilt, like I’m waiting for proof that I’m going to lose everything because I deserve to. I just feel… worthless, I guess. Like I’ve failed in all these roles I’m supposed to be handling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually avoid social situations because you’re afraid people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I usually avoid things for that reason. I mean… I don’t know, I can be uncomfortable wondering how I’m coming across, but I haven’t really thought of it as, like, “I can’t go because they’ll reject me.” It’s more that I get in my head afterward sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start after something specific, or at least it got going around then. It was after I bought my townhouse. That was about… two years and three months ago, roughly.\n\nAt first I thought it would feel like this big adult milestone, like something I should be proud of, and I was, kind of. But pretty quickly the pressure of keeping up with it started building — the payments, the maintenance, making sure nothing went wrong, feeling like if anything slipped it meant I wasn’t handling my life right. And I think that’s when the guilt and the “I’m failing” thoughts really started becoming more constant. It wasn’t like one single day where everything changed, but looking back, that’s the point where it began.\n\nDoctor: Aside from worries about the townhouse and keeping up with it, do you still feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I do. It’s not only the townhouse. That’s part of it, definitely, because it gave my brain this very concrete thing to latch onto, like, “If you can’t keep up with this, you’re failing.” But the feeling is bigger than that.\n\nLike at work, if something goes wrong — even something that probably has multiple reasons or other people involved — I immediately feel like it’s because I did something wrong. Like I’m the weak link, or I’m not competent enough, or everyone is going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then it kind of spreads into this whole judgment of myself, like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, I’m a bad employee, a bad person, someone who doesn’t deserve what I have.\n\nSo, yes, even aside from the townhouse, there’s this… constant guilt, I guess. Like everything that goes wrong gets traced back to me somehow. And I keep feeling like I deserve to lose the things I worked for because maybe I didn’t really earn them, or I’m not handling them well enough. It’s exhausting, honestly, because even when nothing huge is happening, I still feel like I’m waiting for proof that I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think that’s fair to say. It’s been going on a long time now — about two years and one month, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but the depressed feeling, and also just not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been there pretty consistently for that whole stretch. Some days I can function and get through what I need to do, so I almost second-guess whether I should call it “depressed,” but when I look at the pattern, yeah, it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current two-year period, had you ever gone through another distinct stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days or lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I can point to another clear, separate two-week stretch before this where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely the same kind of depressed most days,” or where I clearly lost interest in everything.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times before where I was stressed or unhappy, like anyone, and I’m sure there were rough patches where I didn’t feel great. But if you mean a distinct period that really stands out as depression for at least two weeks, before this current long stretch… I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind in a clear way. This current period is the one that feels obvious and persistent enough that I can recognize it.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed and I’m tired, like I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there awake for hours. I end up listening to every little noise in the house — the walls settling, pipes, whatever small creaks happen — and it feels like I’m just stuck there waiting for sleep to happen.\n\nAnd then even if I do finally fall asleep, I usually wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.\n\nThere are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.\n\nI haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. But no, I’m not sitting there making plans to hurt myself. It’s more these passive thoughts when I’m worn down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “too much,” because a lot of the things I think about feel like they have some reason behind them, even if maybe I go over them more than I need to.\n\nLike, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff or whether I handled something wrong, or just general life things, but I don’t know that it’s across “many different parts” in the way you’re asking. It’s not like I’m constantly worrying about every possible thing all day long for no reason. At least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes I probably overthink, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as this uncontrollable worry about everything. I guess I’d say I have stress and I dwell on things, but I’m not sure it’s the same as what you mean by excessive worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but it’s kind of messy. It hasn’t been one simple thing like “I’m never hungry” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s more like during the day I just don’t really eat proper meals. I’ll have coffee and maybe toast, or something small because it’s easy, and then I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t actually had lunch or anything that counts as real food.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until my clothes feel looser, like my waistband or work pants fitting differently. And then I’m like, okay, I guess I haven’t been eating normally. It’s not intentional exactly — I’m not trying to lose weight — it’s just that making food or deciding what to eat feels like too much, so I end up living on whatever is easiest. Coffee, toast, random snacks, that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I feel tired a lot, but I don’t know if I’d honestly say it’s nearly every day in the way you mean. Some days I can get through work and do what I need to do, even if I’m kind of dragging, and other days I notice it more, like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out whether it’s “low energy” or just being worn down, if that makes sense. I don’t wake up thinking, “I have no energy today,” every single day, but I do have stretches where I feel like I’m moving slower than normal or like normal tasks feel heavier. I don’t know if anyone has said it in exactly those words, like “you’re moving slowly,” but I can feel it, and I think it probably shows.\n\nSome mornings I’ll get to my desk and even basic things feel weirdly heavy and slow. Like opening up spreadsheets, clicking through files, typing out responses to emails—things I normally do without thinking—feel like I’m pushing through wet cement. Even walking to a meeting can feel like it takes more effort than it should, like my body just isn’t keeping up at the usual pace.\n\nI’ve had moments where I’m aware people are waiting for me to answer or finish something, and I’m not trying to be difficult or distracted, it’s just like there’s this delay between knowing what I need to do and actually doing it. So… yes, I think at least at work it’s noticeable sometimes, even if people haven’t directly called it out.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it harder for you to function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’m still functioning, like I’m still going to work and I’m not completely unable to take care of things, but it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nAt work, I’ve started making mistakes that I normally wouldn’t make — little things in reports or missing details in emails or not following through as cleanly on something. And it’s happened enough that my supervisor has started checking in more, like asking how projects are going or wanting to review things more closely. They haven’t threatened anything, but I can tell it’s noticeable, and that makes me feel embarrassed because I’m usually pretty dependable.\n\nAt home it’s similar. I live in a townhouse, and I’ve let it get messier than I can stand. I’ll say yes to something and then when it comes time to go, I just don’t have it in me, or I feel like I can’t pull myself together enough to show up and act normal. So… yes, it’s definitely made basic responsibilities harder. I’m keeping things going, but it feels like I’m doing it with a lot less margin than before.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when these symptoms were present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. I mean, if you’re asking whether there was at least a two-week stretch where it was there almost every day, then yes.
