It hasn’t been identical every single day, like some days are a little more manageable than others, but it’s not like it came and went in just short bursts. There have absolutely been two-week periods where it was basically there every day, and honestly it feels more like it’s just been this long, ongoing thing that I’ve been trying to live around.\n\nDoctor: Are the problems with the townhouse still ongoing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they are. I wish I could say I caught up and got it back to normal, but it’s still kind of ongoing.\n\nIt’s not like the place is unsafe or anything, but it’s messier than I’m comfortable with. There’s laundry that sits there longer than it should, clutter on counters, things I mean to put away and then they just stay where I dropped them. And every time I walk past it, I feel bad about it, but then actually doing it feels like another whole task I have to gear myself up for.\n\nI’m still working, and I’m still handling the basics, but it takes a lot more effort than it used to. It’s the same kind of thing as at work, where I’m keeping up but barely — I’ve made enough mistakes that my supervisor has been checking in more, and at home it feels like I’m always behind. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I would. I mean, the townhouse definitely makes it worse, because it’s like this constant reminder that I’m behind and not handling things the way I used to. So if that eased up, I’d probably feel a little less overwhelmed, maybe less guilty day to day.

But looking back, the low mood and the not really caring about things — or not getting interested in things the way I used to — it hasn’t only been about the townhouse. It’s been there for almost the whole time since this started. There have been little stretches where I functioned a bit better, like I could push through more or keep up a little more, but they were brief. It didn’t feel like the depression actually lifted; it was more like I had a short window where I could manage around it better.

So… yes, even if the townhouse stress got better for a while, I think the depressed feeling would still be there. Maybe quieter, but still there.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F41.1, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t get out from under this feeling that everything that goes wrong is somehow my fault. Like at work, if something is delayed or someone’s unhappy, my brain immediately goes to, “You messed this up, you’re not good at your job, you’re a bad employee.” And then at home it’s the same thing — if there’s a problem with the house or something I can’t keep up with, I start thinking I’m a bad homeowner too, like I don’t deserve to have the things I’ve worked for.\n\nIt sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but it’s like this constant guilt, like I’m waiting for proof that I’m going to lose everything because I deserve to. I just feel… worthless, I guess. Like I’ve failed in all these roles I’m supposed to be handling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually avoid social situations because you’re afraid people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I usually avoid things for that reason. I mean… I don’t know, I can be uncomfortable wondering how I’m coming across, but I haven’t really thought of it as, like, “I can’t go because they’ll reject me.” It’s more that I get in my head afterward sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start after something specific, or at least it got going around then. It was after I bought my townhouse. That was about… two years and three months ago, roughly.\n\nAt first I thought it would feel like this big adult milestone, like something I should be proud of, and I was, kind of. But pretty quickly the pressure of keeping up with it started building — the payments, the maintenance, making sure nothing went wrong, feeling like if anything slipped it meant I wasn’t handling my life right. And I think that’s when the guilt and the “I’m failing” thoughts really started becoming more constant. It wasn’t like one single day where everything changed, but looking back, that’s the point where it began.\n\nDoctor: Aside from worries about the townhouse and keeping up with it, do you still feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I do. It’s not only the townhouse. That’s part of it, definitely, because it gave my brain this very concrete thing to latch onto, like, “If you can’t keep up with this, you’re failing.” But the feeling is bigger than that.\n\nLike at work, if something goes wrong — even something that probably has multiple reasons or other people involved — I immediately feel like it’s because I did something wrong. Like I’m the weak link, or I’m not competent enough, or everyone is going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then it kind of spreads into this whole judgment of myself, like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, I’m a bad employee, a bad person, someone who doesn’t deserve what I have.\n\nSo, yes, even aside from the townhouse, there’s this… constant guilt, I guess. Like everything that goes wrong gets traced back to me somehow. And I keep feeling like I deserve to lose the things I worked for because maybe I didn’t really earn them, or I’m not handling them well enough. It’s exhausting, honestly, because even when nothing huge is happening, I still feel like I’m waiting for proof that I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think that’s fair to say. It’s been going on a long time now — about two years and one month, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but the depressed feeling, and also just not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been there pretty consistently for that whole stretch. Some days I can function and get through what I need to do, so I almost second-guess whether I should call it “depressed,” but when I look at the pattern, yeah, it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current two-year period, had you ever gone through another distinct stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days or lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I can point to another clear, separate two-week stretch before this where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely the same kind of depressed most days,” or where I clearly lost interest in everything.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times before where I was stressed or unhappy, like anyone, and I’m sure there were rough patches where I didn’t feel great. But if you mean a distinct period that really stands out as depression for at least two weeks, before this current long stretch… I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind in a clear way. This current period is the one that feels obvious and persistent enough that I can recognize it.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed and I’m tired, like I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there awake for hours. I end up listening to every little noise in the house — the walls settling, pipes, whatever small creaks happen — and it feels like I’m just stuck there waiting for sleep to happen.\n\nAnd then even if I do finally fall asleep, I usually wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.\n\nThere are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.\n\nI haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. But no, I’m not sitting there making plans to hurt myself. It’s more these passive thoughts when I’m worn down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “too much,” because a lot of the things I think about feel like they have some reason behind them, even if maybe I go over them more than I need to.\n\nLike, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff or whether I handled something wrong, or just general life things, but I don’t know that it’s across “many different parts” in the way you’re asking. It’s not like I’m constantly worrying about every possible thing all day long for no reason. At least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes I probably overthink, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as this uncontrollable worry about everything. I guess I’d say I have stress and I dwell on things, but I’m not sure it’s the same as what you mean by excessive worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but it’s kind of messy. It hasn’t been one simple thing like “I’m never hungry” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s more like during the day I just don’t really eat proper meals. I’ll have coffee and maybe toast, or something small because it’s easy, and then I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t actually had lunch or anything that counts as real food.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until my clothes feel looser, like my waistband or work pants fitting differently. And then I’m like, okay, I guess I haven’t been eating normally. It’s not intentional exactly — I’m not trying to lose weight — it’s just that making food or deciding what to eat feels like too much, so I end up living on whatever is easiest. Coffee, toast, random snacks, that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I feel tired a lot, but I don’t know if I’d honestly say it’s nearly every day in the way you mean. Some days I can get through work and do what I need to do, even if I’m kind of dragging, and other days I notice it more, like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nIt’s hard to separate out whether it’s “low energy” or just being worn down, if that makes sense. I don’t wake up thinking, “I have no energy today,” every single day, but I do have stretches where I feel like I’m moving slower than normal or like normal tasks feel heavier. But I haven’t really been tracking it closely enough to say yes, definitely nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe people have noticed, especially at work. I don’t know if anyone has said it in exactly those words, like “you’re moving slowly,” but I can feel it, and I think it probably shows.\n\nSome mornings I’ll get to my desk and even basic things feel weirdly heavy and slow. Like opening up spreadsheets, clicking through files, typing out responses to emails—things I normally do without thinking—feel like I’m pushing through wet cement. Even walking to a meeting can feel like it takes more effort than it should, like my body just isn’t keeping up at the usual pace.\n\nI’ve had moments where I’m aware people are waiting for me to answer or finish something, and I’m not trying to be difficult or distracted, it’s just like there’s this delay between knowing what I need to do and actually doing it. So… yes, I think at least at work it’s noticeable sometimes, even if people haven’t directly called it out.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it harder for you to function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’m still functioning, like I’m still going to work and I’m not completely unable to take care of things, but it takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nAt work, I’ve started making mistakes that I normally wouldn’t make — little things in reports or missing details in emails or not following through as cleanly on something. And it’s happened enough that my supervisor has started checking in more, like asking how projects are going or wanting to review things more closely. They haven’t threatened anything, but I can tell it’s noticeable, and that makes me feel embarrassed because I’m usually pretty dependable.\n\nAt home it’s similar. I live in a townhouse, and I’ve let it get messier than I can stand. Not like one bad day of dishes, more like piles of laundry, clutter, things staying where I dropped them because I can’t make myself deal with it right away. And then it bothers me every time I look at it, but actually fixing it feels like this huge project.\n\nI’ve also missed social plans, which isn’t like me. I’ll say yes to something and then when it comes time to go, I just don’t have it in me, or I feel like I can’t pull myself together enough to show up and act normal. So… yes, it’s definitely made basic responsibilities harder. I’m keeping things going, but it feels like I’m doing it with a lot less margin than before.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when these symptoms were present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. I mean, if you’re asking whether there was at least a two-week stretch where it was there almost every day, then yes. It’s been much longer than that, honestly.\n\nThe depressed feeling and the not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been going on for about two years and maybe a month now. It hasn’t been identical every single day, like some days are a little more manageable than others, but it’s not like it came and went in just short bursts. There have absolutely been two-week periods where it was basically there every day, and honestly it feels more like it’s just been this long, ongoing thing that I’ve been trying to live around.\n\nDoctor: Are the problems with the townhouse still ongoing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they are. I wish I could say I caught up and got it back to normal, but it’s still kind of ongoing.\n\nIt’s not like the place is unsafe or anything, but it’s messier than I’m comfortable with. There’s laundry that sits there longer than it should, clutter on counters, things I mean to put away and then they just stay where I dropped them. And every time I walk past it, I feel bad about it, but then actually doing it feels like another whole task I have to gear myself up for.\n\nI’m still working, and I’m still handling the basics, but it takes a lot more effort than it used to. It’s the same kind of thing as at work, where I’m keeping up but barely — I’ve made enough mistakes that my supervisor has been checking in more, and at home it feels like I’m always behind. I’ve missed social plans too, because by the time I’m supposed to go, I feel like I can’t pull myself together and also deal with everything else.\n\nSo, yes… the townhouse stuff is still happening. It’s one of the things that makes me feel like I’m not really managing the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Before this current two-year period, did you ever have a past episode when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I can point to a clear past episode like that before this one.\n\nI’ve had times before where I was stressed or down for a few days, or maybe I wasn’t myself for a bit, but I honestly don’t know that it was “most of the day nearly every day” for two weeks or more. Nothing stands out in my memory the way this current period does. This feels different because it’s been so persistent and hard to get out from under.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I don’t think so, but I’m not completely confident because I didn’t really track it that closely before.\n\nDoctor: If the townhouse stress eased for a while, would you still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, the townhouse definitely makes it worse, because it’s like this constant reminder that I’m behind and not handling things the way I used to. So if that eased up, I’d probably feel a little less overwhelmed, maybe less guilty day to day.\n\nBut looking back, the low mood and the not really caring about things — or not getting interested in things the way I used to — it hasn’t only been about the townhouse. It’s been there for almost the whole time since this started. There have been little stretches where I functioned a bit better, like I could push through more or keep up a little more, but they were brief. It didn’t feel like the depression actually lifted; it was more like I had a short window where I could manage around it better.\n\nSo… yes, even if the townhouse stress got better for a while, I think the depressed feeling would still be there. Maybe quieter, but still there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates":["F32.9","F43.2"],"note":"Sustained depressed mood/anhedonia for >2 years with insomnia, appetite/weight loss, psychomotor slowing, guilt/worthlessness, passive death wishes, and functional impairment supports a current major depressive episode. Prior distinct depressive episodes are not established, so recurrent depression is less likely and was removed. Worry is not clearly excessive/generalized across domains, so GAD is less supported and was removed. Symptoms began around a stressor but persist beyond it and would continue even if it improved, so adjustment disorder remains a less likely alternative.","is_final":false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, looking back over the last two years, it’s hard for me to separate everything perfectly, because the townhouse stuff has been there on and off and it kind of blends into the whole picture. But there were definitely times when the townhouse wasn’t the main thing on my mind — like it wasn’t actively bothering me that day or that week as much — and I still felt low.

It wasn’t like, “Oh, if the house is less stressful, then I’m suddenly back to myself.” The low mood was still kind of sitting there underneath. And the not being interested in things, or not really feeling pulled toward things I used to care about, that was still there too. I might function a little better for a short stretch, like get through work a bit more smoothly or keep up with a couple things, but it didn’t feel like the depression was gone. It was more like I had a little more room to push through it.

So yes, there were periods where the townhouse stress wasn’t bothering me as much, but I still felt depressed. It’s been there for most of this whole time, just with some brief times where I could manage a little better.

And I think that’s when the guilt and the “I’m failing” thoughts really started becoming more constant. It wasn’t like one single day where everything changed, but looking back, that’s the point where it began.\n\nDoctor: Aside from worries about the townhouse and keeping up with it, do you still feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I do. It’s not only the townhouse. That’s part of it, definitely, because it gave my brain this very concrete thing to latch onto, like, “If you can’t keep up with this, you’re failing.” But the feeling is bigger than that.\n\nLike at work, if something goes wrong — even something that probably has multiple reasons or other people involved — I immediately feel like it’s because I did something wrong. Like I’m the weak link, or I’m not competent enough, or everyone is going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then it kind of spreads into this whole judgment of myself, like I’m not just someone who made a mistake, I’m a bad employee, a bad person, someone who doesn’t deserve what I have.\n\nSo, yes, even aside from the townhouse, there’s this… constant guilt, I guess. Like everything that goes wrong gets traced back to me somehow. And I keep feeling like I deserve to lose the things I worked for because maybe I didn’t really earn them, or I’m not handling them well enough. It’s exhausting, honestly, because even when nothing huge is happening, I still feel like I’m waiting for proof that I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think that’s fair to say. It’s been going on a long time now — about two years and one month, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but the depressed feeling, and also just not really being interested in things the way I used to be… that’s been there pretty consistently for that whole stretch. Some days I can function and get through what I need to do, so I almost second-guess whether I should call it “depressed,” but when I look at the pattern, yeah, it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current two-year period, had you ever gone through another distinct stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days or lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I can point to another clear, separate two-week stretch before this where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely the same kind of depressed most days,” or where I clearly lost interest in everything.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times before where I was stressed or unhappy, like anyone, and I’m sure there were rough patches where I didn’t feel great. But if you mean a distinct period that really stands out as depression for at least two weeks, before this current long stretch… I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind in a clear way. This current period is the one that feels obvious and persistent enough that I can recognize it.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… really bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed and I’m tired, like I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there awake for hours. I end up listening to every little noise in the house — the walls settling, pipes, whatever small creaks happen — and it feels like I’m just stuck there waiting for sleep to happen.\n\nAnd then even if I do finally fall asleep, I usually wake up around three or four in the morning. It’s like my body just snaps awake, and then I can’t really get back to sleep before work. I’ll lie there trying not to look at the clock, but I know I have to be up soon, and then that makes it worse. So, yeah, it’s most nights.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. And I don’t want to say something too dramatic if it’s not accurate.\n\nThere are times when I’m lying awake or when I’m really exhausted where I think, like… I don’t know, “I don’t want to keep doing this,” or “it would be easier to just not have to wake up and deal with everything.” But that feels different from me actively wanting to hurt myself, if that makes sense. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific intention.\n\nI haven’t really talked about it much because even saying it out loud sounds worse than it feels in my head sometimes. But no, I’m not sitting there making plans to hurt myself. It’s more these passive thoughts when I’m worn down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things, but it’s hard for me to tell what counts as “too much,” because a lot of the things I think about feel like they have some reason behind them, even if maybe I go over them more than I need to.\n\nLike, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff or whether I handled something wrong, or just general life things, but I don’t know that it’s across “many different parts” in the way you’re asking. It’s not like I’m constantly worrying about every possible thing all day long for no reason. At least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes I probably overthink, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as this uncontrollable worry about everything. I guess I’d say I have stress and I dwell on things, but I’m not sure it’s the same as what you mean by excessive worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but it’s kind of messy. It hasn’t been one simple thing like “I’m never hungry” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s more like during the day I just don’t really eat proper meals. I’ll have coffee and maybe toast, or something small because it’s easy, and then I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t actually had lunch or anything that counts as real food.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until my clothes feel looser, like my waistband or work pants fitting differently. And then I’m like, okay, I guess I haven’t been eating normally. It’s not intentional exactly — I’m not trying to lose weight — it’s just that making food or deciding what to eat feels like too much, so I end up living on whatever is easiest.
